Every unpredictable row is in the 119th Congress, so "in all" differs from "counted" only for Trump's second term. For that row the block gives the exact text, "0 counted vetoes; 2 declared unpredictable", which shows the counted figure and the all-rows figure side by side. No printed "in all" figure goes into any rate, expected count, V_COUNT, M, bootstrap or decisive value (:1311-1318, :1449). So the outcome is the same whichever way the general clause is read. That meets "already handled", and fidelity to H2 ("never counted for or against", :727) holds. What remains is wording: "The ONE exception" (:770) overstates, because the block itself lists the other all-rows mentions at :1308-1322 and :1438, and :790's "US-10's 34 is its count over all rows" is another one. That makes it a low clarity item at most. If anyone acts on it, a one-line fix is enough: point COUNTED ONLY to the list in TRUMP'S 2025 VETOES (:1311-1322) and to the digest's unpredictable lines.

### fidelity-F5 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** roll calls stay in the fit (H1: "the recorded chamber-by-chamber votes since 2017"; R16

**Defect.** The block cites H1's fit phrase to keep the budget-class roll calls in the fit. It then fits only "every distinct roll call on which the PP of its OWNER has bloc stance NAY", which drops every roll call where the PP stood YEA, and those are most of the recorded votes since 2017. Restricting the fit to the stance-conditional subset is a defensible reading, but it narrows H1's words and is nowhere declared as a reading of them. R2 (every contested roll call) exists but is not presented as the alternative reading of H1's fit base. The same phrase is used to widen the fit in one clause and is silently narrowed in the next.

**Evidence.** H1 (l.726): "fitted on the recorded chamber-by-chamber votes since 2017". Block l.905-906: "THE FITTED ROLL CALLS, per chamber: every distinct roll call on which the PP of its OWNER has bloc stance NAY." Block l.1360-1363: R2.

**Fix proposed.** Add under THE FIT: "DECLARED READING OF H1's fit base: 'the recorded chamber-by-chamber votes since 2017' is read as the recorded roll calls on which the PP stood NAY, the stance in which a draw is made. R2 is the wider reading (every contested roll call)." Keep the budget-class justification consistent with that reading.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: This is not a medium defect. At most it is a labelling nit, and the block already covers the substance. (1) The quote is accurate and the reference is true. Plan l.898 quotes H1 at l.726 word for word. It is used for one purpose only: to say that RECORD BUDGET CLASS roll calls are not dropped from the fit, even though H1 keeps budget votes out of the draw. It does not widen the fit beyond the PP-NAY set. Budget-class roll calls on which the PP stood NAY stay in the fitted set at l.905-906, so the two clauses agree. (2) There is nothing two implementers would read differently. The fitted set is exact at l.905-906: "every distinct roll call on which the PP of its OWNER has bloc stance NAY". The stance split is stated as the model's structure in three places: - THE TERMS l.838-839: "THE CELLS: HP and SP, the PP standing NAY ... HO and SO, the OP standing YEA on those same roll calls" - MODEL item 2, l.855-856: the PP standing NAY draws from HP/SP - MODEL item 7, l.879-880: "split by chamber, role and stance only (four cells)" So the cells are fitted on the stance in which the draw is made, and this is declared openly, not silently. (3) The wider fit base is already a declared alternative. R2 at l.1360-1363 reads "the fit over every contested roll call ... These are the cells a draw on every contested ordinary bill would use". Every declared reading "runs the whole rule with one change; outcome and headline figures printed" (l.1355-1356). Its outcome is named in the verdict line whenever it differs (l.1245-1246, l.1256-1258). The block's own frame says each contestable default carries "a reading printed beside it and named in the verdict line where its outcome differs" (l.1410-1412). R2 does not carry the words "reading of H1's fit base", but it is plainly the alternative fit base. (4) H1's phrase does not require every recorded vote to be an observation. COMPLETED.md §803 (l.~37966) and plan l.723 show that "chamber-by-chamber" answers s802's STOP: it means the record read as members voted in each chamber, instead of the one-stance premise. It names the data and how to read it, not an every-vote estimator. The reviewer's own fix also calls R2 "the wider reading" of the literal words, yet R2 also leaves out uncontested votes. So the proposed label would be no more faithful to H1 than the current text.

### form-F3 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** COMPLETED.md section 832, specified by a design panel blind to every figure of the rule.

**Defect.** The block will be frozen, yet it carries a claim about how it was written and three references to a panel that a reader who never saw the panel cannot resolve: 'the panel's default' (twice) and 'the panel's addition'. If a later review finds the panel saw some figure, as §794 recorded for R-US19's panel, this sentence can only be corrected by an amendment that needs Elias's ruling. R-US18 and R-US19 keep their provenance outside the markers, in a paragraph before the block that also says 'everything outside the markers - the ruling record's mirror, the asks - is not part of it'. R-US17 has no such paragraph. Its block runs straight on from H1-H3.

**Evidence.** In the block: 'made and (a) never fires (the panel's default, by analogy to Poland's G5 items 14-15).'; 'The debt-limit act is the panel's default'; '- S3, TOO THIN (the panel's addition, not one of R-US17's two STOPs)'. The plan's 'The rule, declared before the instrument's first run (§791)' and '(§794)' paragraphs sit before the R-US18 and R-US19 blocks. Around R-US17, lines 725-729 contain only H1-H3 and the BEGIN marker.

**Fix proposed.** Cut the header to: "R-US17'S RULE (c) - Elias's rulings H1-H3: ... Preregistered in COMPLETED.md section 832." Replace 'the panel's default' with 'DECLARED default' (both places) and 'the panel's addition' with 'added by this rule'. Before the BEGIN marker, add a paragraph in R-US18's form that states the provenance (the blind panel, its brief, the audit in §832) and says that the mirror line and the digests line outside the markers are not part of the rule.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quotes are accurate, and the comparison with R-US18 and R-US19 is accurate. R-US18's block (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1518-1519) and R-US19's block (:1758-1759) have no "panel" wording inside the markers. Their provenance sits in the paragraphs at :1514 and :1754. R-US17's block does carry it at :734, :783, :833 and :1223, and :725-729 has no such paragraph. But this is a difference in form from the precedents. It is not a defect of the rule at medium. (1) No two honest implementers would read it differently. "The panel's default" only says who made a choice. The working text around it is complete: "no draw is made and (a) never fires" (:782-783); "The debt-limit act is the panel's default ... with R18 printed" (:833-834) makes the act a BUDGET VOTE by default with reading R18; S3's trigger is fully stated at :1223-1225. A reader who never saw the panel can resolve "the panel" from the header at :734. The block also says at :1410-1412 that H1-H3, G1, G5, E4 and H4 settle every choice and "Each remaining choice is a default with its reason", which places these defaults. (2) It is not a false reference. "Preregistered in COMPLETED.md section 832" points forward, like R-US18's "section 791"; §832 does not exist yet (COMPLETED.md ends at §831, :38426). "Blind to every figure of the rule" means what the brief means by "figure of the new rule" (facts.md:15, :106): an output of rule (c). Inputs such as B1's 45/166 do not count. No output has been computed. The worry that a later review might find the panel saw a figure is hypothetical. Even then, a correction could be recorded in COMPLETED.md without touching the digest, and the header sentence feeds no computation, assertion or decision. (3) The scope of the frozen text is already handled inside the block. The digest covers only "the lines strictly between the marker lines" (:1417-1421). The digests line is "the plan's 'The block's digests' line" (:1422-1423). The mirror is "the line after the block" (:1470-1475). Lines :738-740 put US-21's own items "outside this block". So the requested statement that the mirror and digests lines are not part of the rule is already made. At most this is a low-severity clarity or consistency suggestion: an optional provenance paragraph before the BEGIN marker. It is not a medium defect.

### form-F4 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.**     digest: "**Ruling record:** INSTALLED-PENDING-E4 (sNNN): (c)." /
    "**Ruling record:** INSTALLED (sNNN; E4 sMMM): (c)." /
    "**Ruling record:** ASKED (sNNN): S1 + S2." /
    "**Ruling record:** RULED (sNNN): <option>."

**Defect.** The mirror forms the instrument compares are written with 'sNNN'. The plan's mirror lines, and the precedent instrument's code, write the section with the section sign. The block's ASCII rule forces 's', and the block defines 'sNNN' only as 'the COMPLETED.md section a later commit takes', so a literal implementer would build '(s840)'. That breaks R-US18's form ('ASKED (§800)') and the current mirror 'NONE (§832)'. The same problem affects RuleDigests' first entry, 'preregistered (s832)', against the precedent's '"preregistered (§791)"'. The block also gives no NONE form, while the plan's line after the block reads '**Ruling record:** NONE (§832) - the instrument is not built; its first run registers the outcome.', which matches none of the four forms. And the ASKED form hard-codes one outcome, 'S1 + S2', where S3, S1 or S2 alone are equally possible.

**Evidence.** UsHouseCountCheck.cs line 40 has `RulingSection = "§800"`, line 690 builds the mirror as `"**Ruling record:** {0} ({1}): {2}."`, and line 33 is `"preregistered (§791)"`. In the plan, line 1503 is the NONE line and line 1730 is R-US18's mirror 'ASKED (§800): ...'.

**Fix proposed.** Add to THE MIRROR: "In the mirror line and in RuleDigests, sNNN is written as the plan writes a section: the section sign (U+00A7) followed by the number. The ASKED form is "**Ruling record:** ASKED (sNNN): <outcome>.", with <outcome> spelled as THE OUTCOME spells it. Under NONE the line reads "**Ruling record:** NONE (s832)" and may carry text after it. It is not checked, since no instrument exists."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: This is not a medium defect. Every part of the claim is either already handled by the block or wrong about the consequence. At most it is a low clarity nit. (1) The 's' in sNNN. Lines 737-738 define "sNNN" as a placeholder that "stand[s] for the COMPLETED.md section a later commit takes". It is not literal text. The block writes 's' for the section sign only because of its own ASCII rule. Line 1418 applies that rule to THE BLOCK alone ("in plain ASCII inside a ```text fence"). The block plainly uses 's' as an ASCII stand-in for the sign: "P.L. 94-329 s601(b)" and "D.C. Code s1-206.04" at line 811, and "COMPLETED.md section 832" at line 734 next to "s832" at line 1422. The mirror line sits outside the block, where the ASCII rule does not apply. The exact line the instrument will compare was written at the preregistering commit as "**Ruling record:** NONE (§832)" (line 1503), which shows how this line writes a section. The C# precedent uses the sign too (UsHouseCountCheck.cs:33 "preregistered (§791)" and :40 RulingSection = "§800"). An implementer following either one writes §. Even under a literal reading, nothing mis-decides. The check is equality between the string the instrument builds and the plan line (UsHouseCountCheck.cs:690-691 pattern), and the first-run commit authors both. RuleDigests' Why text is only a label, used in a failure message (UsHouseCountCheck.cs:582) and never compared. The claim that this "breaks R-US18's form ('ASKED (§800)')" is false. R-US18's mirror (line 1730) is checked only by UsHouseCountCheck against its own RulingSection, and R-US17's spelling cannot reach it. (2) No NONE form. None is needed. Lines 1451-1452 say the instrument fails while it reads NONE and the first-run commit registers the outcome. Lines 1496-1497 require that commit to register INSTALLED-PENDING-E4 or ASKED. So the NONE line at 1503 is never compared to anything, and the proposed fix concedes this ("It is not checked, since no instrument exists"). (3) The ASKED form "S1 + S2". It is one of four examples separated by "/". Line 1456 defines ASKED as "with the outcome and the digest", and line 1470 says the mirror "mirrors the state". Line 1237 spells that outcome "S1 + S2" exactly as the example does. No honest implementer would mirror "S1 + S2" for an S3 run, since that line would not mirror the record and the instrument would have to fail by its own rule at line 1475.

### form-F6 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.**     preregistered digest), the catalog, the document and the mutation suite; the cheap bar

**Defect.** The first-run commit's bars list the mutation suite only as a file to add. They never require running it: no case count, and no control reproducing the document and the catalog byte for byte. A first-run commit could pass every bar listed here with a suite that was never run. THE MUTATION SUITE section says what each case must do, but the bars, which decide whether the commit lands, do not ask for it.

**Evidence.** In the precedents, a tool's suite count is a reported bar beside the cheap and simulation bars: §831 'The suite re-run (suite831): the control passes ... 13 of 13'; §792 'us_senate_mutations.sh 39 of 39 on the final tool, its control reproducing the repository's bytes'. The block's own WHAT STAYS AS IT IS requires that Tools/us_veto_mutations.sh 'stays 13 of 13'.

**Fix proposed.** Add to THE FIRST-RUN COMMIT: "Tools/us_veto_draw_mutations.sh run on the final code, every case caught by its own message (<k> of <k>), its control reproducing US_VETO_DRAW.md and UsVetoDefections.cs byte for byte, read off its log."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The requirement the claim says is missing is already in the block, inside its own bars section. The section heading at docs/specs/USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1415 is "THE RULING RECORD AND THE BARS". Under that heading, THE MUTATION SUITE bullet at :1476-1477 says "Each case must fail the check with its own message, and the control reproduces the document and the catalog byte for byte". Its case list at :1479-1487 ends with "planted runs proving the UNFITTED, S3, S2 (both cases) and S1 paths". That is a binding "must" about results you only get by running the suite. When the first-run commit at :1492-1493 lists "the mutation suite", it means the suite that bullet defines, pass conditions included. Two claims in the defect are therefore wrong: "no control reproducing the document and the catalog byte for byte" (the control is at :1477), and "a first-run commit could pass every bar listed here with a suite that was never run" (a suite that was never run cannot show that each case fails with its own message, so the commit would fail the block). No honest implementer would read the block as allowing an unrun suite. The precedent cited does not make a count line part of the bars either. In COMPLETED.md §831 (lines ~38452-38456), "The suite re-run (suite831) ... 13 of 13" sits under **Measured**, not **Bars**, which lists only the cheap and simulation bars. Only §792/§795/§797 put the count "beside" the bars. What remains is that no "<k> of <k>" reporting line exists and the block does not say the suite runs "on the final code". That is at most a low clarity point, not a medium ambiguity or a defect in the rule.

### game-F3 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** - POLAND'S B1/F3 (H1: "proved unchanged on the simulation bar"):

**Defect.** H1 asks for Poland to be proved unchanged on the simulation bar, but the checks the block lists for that proof are not on it. PresidentialVetoDiagnostic and GovernmentBudgetBillDiagnostic are in CheckSuite's cheap Suite, not its Simulation table. The only simulation-bar item named is the trajectory sentinel, a no-policy dump. That dump meets no budget bill and no statute through PresidentialVetoGate, so it cannot detect any change to B1/F3. The same gap applies to 'THE FIVE OTHER COUNTRIES': 'the draw counter reads 0 on every non-US path the diagnostic runs' never names which paths. A green simulation bar would be cited as Poland's proof while proving nothing about the veto.

**Evidence.** CheckSuite.cs: PresidentialVetoDiagnostic and GovernmentBudgetBillDiagnostic sit in 'Suite'. The 'Simulation' table holds TrajectorySentinelCheck and neither diagnostic. COMPLETED.md s768: 'budget bills exist for the player's country alone, so the no-policy dump meets none'. US-20's done-when: 'the no-policy dump meets no budget bill, s768, so the dump diff alone proves nothing here'.

**Fix proposed.** Say which bar proves what. Either add PresidentialVetoDiagnostic (its D4/E2 gate cases) to CheckSuite's Simulation table in the first-run commit, so Poland's veto runs on the simulation bar as H1 says. Or state the reading that H1's 'simulation bar' is tier SIMULATION, meaning the cheap and simulation bars together, with the sentinel cited only as 'no world moved'. Name the non-US paths that US-21's diagnostic drives: a planted contested ordinary bill for each of SE, DE, FR, IT and PL through every ordinary-bill vote site, with divisions byte-identical and UsVeto's counter at 0.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The facts the claim cites are right. CheckSuite.cs:202 and :219 put GovernmentBudgetBillDiagnostic and PresidentialVetoDiagnostic in the cheap `Suite`, and the `Simulation` table (CheckSuite.cs:475-572) holds TrajectorySentinelCheck but neither diagnostic. The conclusion still does not follow, for four reasons. (1) The block does not lean on the sentinel. POLAND'S B1/F3 (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1295-1302) names four proofs: the three files byte-identical with an empty git diff, PresidentialVetoGate's body untouched, "PresidentialVetoDiagnostic with its D4 and E2 cases, and GovernmentBudgetBillDiagnostic's Poland pins, pass unmoved", and "The sentinel holds". It ends "All of this is required in the first-run commit and again at US-21's." (2) THE FIRST-RUN COMMIT (:1492-1497) requires "the cheap bar with UsVetoDrawCheck added" alongside "the simulation bar". The two diagnostics run on the cheap bar, so they are already required by name. A green simulation bar alone cannot be passed off as Poland's proof. (3) H1's phrase is read in the project's own terms. CLAUDE.md:59 says "simulation -> the cheap and simulation bars". The same phrase in G6 ("Poland shown unchanged on the simulation bar", plan :2041) was closed at COMPLETED.md s798 by the cheap bar 100 of 100, the named instruments `us20named` (GovernmentBudgetBillDiagnostic, PresidentialVetoDiagnostic) and PresidentialVetoDiagnostic's 73 lines identical, with the simulation bar cited only as "the trajectory sentinel holding ... nothing in a world moved". The block's "the simulation bar (Poland's proof ..., H1)" follows that precedent. Two honest implementers would run the same checks, and nothing mis-decides. (4) The five-country part is not ambiguity that matters. Inertness is guaranteed in the rule's own structure: "UsVeto.Applies is true for CountryId.USA alone, asserted over all six values" (:1289), and THE MODEL makes no draw for "anything outside CountryId.USA" (:859). The unnamed "non-US path the diagnostic runs" belongs to US-21's diagnostic design (:1346-1349, :1498-1499). At most that is a low-severity clarity nit, not a medium defect. Moving the diagnostics into the Simulation table would add no proof the block does not already require.

### game-F4 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** THE INSTRUMENT: a new Editor check, Assets/Editor/UsVetoDrawCheck.cs, in the cheap and
documents groups.

**Defect.** The block sets no time budget or run split, yet asks the cheap and documents groups to compute everything. That is B = 2000 percentile-bootstrap replicates, each scoring every measure of V on LOO cells. THE PRINT puts a BOOT interval on every FITTED figure 'under the default or any reading', so each reading's six headline figures need their own 2000 replicates too. R17 adds a second bootstrap; INFLUENCE adds one exact recompute per fitted roll call; and there are 1000 seeded realizations. P(pass) is defined as an enumeration over index pairs, about n_PP x n_OP per chamber per measure. Under R2 (every contested roll call) the cells can hold hundreds of observations, so a literal implementation does n^2 x |V| x 2000 work per reading. The cheap bar is about 212 s for 103 checks, it also runs on every Editor open (CheckSuite.ScheduleOnEditorOpen), and the mutation suite pays the cost again in each Unity launch.

**Evidence.** CheckSuite.cs: '[InitializeOnLoadMethod] ... RunAll' once per session, and RunDocumentBatch requires every documents check to be in the cheap suite. Block: 'B = 2000 (record resampling)', 'PER-MEASURE figures: every measure j of the full V is scored from the replicate's cells, LOO', 'A FITTED FIGURE is any number computed ... under the default or any reading ... on EVERY surface', R2's union of contested roll calls. GeneratedCatalogCheck's precedent: 'It compares the DIGEST of the input, not a re-parse'.

**Fix proposed.** State a budget and a split. The cheap and documents run (Run) computes only the exact decision chain (cells, LOO P(sent) and P(named), the totals, the stops, the decisive digest, the catalog) plus the identities. The BOOT intervals, the readings' BOOT, R17, INFLUENCE and the realizations are computed only by the Write entry point. The document's stamp carries a digest of their inputs (SourceDigest, block digest, and the blobs of UsVeto.cs and UsVetoDrawCheck.cs), which Run re-derives and compares. Require the K-histogram or cumulative form ('Histograms of K may implement this') wherever a cell has more than 50 observations, and record the measured check time in the first-run commit's bars.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: This is a performance and engineering concern, not a defect of the rule. The reviewer's quotes of the block are accurate: BOOT B = 2000 (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1064-1078), the label on every FITTED figure under any reading (1045-1049, 1212-1213), R17 (1388-1390), INFLUENCE (1208-1211) and 1000 realizations (1124-1131). But none of the severity tests applies. (1) Nothing gets decided differently. The work in question produces no different figure, outcome or digest. THE DECISION reads only the exact LOO chain (1219-1220), and the decisive digest leaves out every interval, reading, realization and INFLUENCE result (1448-1449). Two implementers who organise the work differently write the same document, catalog and digest. So this is not "ambiguity two honest implementers would resolve differently" in anything the rule decides. (2) The n^2 cost is already handled. The rule defines P(pass) as an enumeration, but line 1122-1123 says "Histograms of K may implement this but must equal the enumeration". A literal pair-by-pair implementation is allowed, not required. (3) The block does not forbid the proposed split. It only names the check and its groups (1139-1142). The only in-memory regeneration it requires is the catalog (1337), and that needs only the cells. "Asserted" (736) means the instrument fails while the statement is false. A Write entry point that asserts, plus a Run that re-derives an input digest, meets that, so the split needs no amendment. (4) Project precedent puts this at build time, not in a frozen block. Existing instruments use the Run/WriteReadings split (UsNationalVoteCheck, COMPLETED.md:37546; UsHouseCountCheck, plan:322), and its measured cost is recorded in the bars ("9.6 s of it", COMPLETED.md:37565). Neither R-US18's block (plan:1516-1728) nor R-US19's (1756-1993) sets a time budget. The documents group already accepts a heavy check: "two eve worlds, so a card-only commit pays for them" (CheckSuite.cs, Documents table). (5) It is computable at this size. Per US_VETO_BACKTEST.md there are 549 House and 175 Senate final roll calls. The default cells hold only roll calls where the president's party stands NAY, and V is about the size of F3's 34. Even R2's cells number in the low hundreds. That is seconds to tens of seconds of integer comparisons, nowhere near uncomputable. ScheduleOnEditorOpen runs once per session on delayCall and is skipped in batch mode (CheckSuite.cs:371-382). Writing a time budget into the frozen block would only constrain implementation details that the rule rightly leaves to the first-run commit.

### game-F5 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** - It writes docs/generated/US_VETO_DRAW.md, stamped with SourceDigest, the pins, the
  block's digest, the decisive digest and the ruling-record state.
- It writes the catalog (HOW THE FIGURES REACH THE GAME).

**Defect.** The block does not say whether the cheap-bar check writes. 'It writes' the document and the catalog, yet 'The cheap bar regenerates the catalog in memory and fails on any byte of difference'. One implementer writes on every Run, which rewrites a .cs file under Assets mid-bar: an asset refresh and recompile inside the suite, and a check that always passes against itself. Another compares in Run and writes from a separate entry point. For the document, no staleness test is stated at all. The existing pattern (UsHouseCountCheck.Run compares and fails 'STALE - regenerate'; WriteReadings writes and is 'refused while any check fails') is not named.

**Evidence.** Assets/Editor/UsHouseCountCheck.cs: Run checks 'onDisk == expected', and WriteReadings does 'File.WriteAllText(path, written, ...)' only when failures == 0. Block, HOW THE FIGURES REACH THE GAME: 'The cheap bar regenerates the catalog in memory and fails on any byte of difference'.

**Fix proposed.** Replace both lines with: 'Run (cheap and documents groups) never writes. It regenerates US_VETO_DRAW.md and Generated/UsVetoDefections.cs in memory and fails on any byte of difference, naming the entry point to run. UsVetoDrawCheck.Write writes both and is refused while any check fails (UsHouseCountCheck.WriteReadings' form).' Add a mutation case: an edited document.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The block already covers the failure the reviewer describes. What is left is an implementation detail, not a defect of the rule. (1) Catalog. The block does say whether the cheap-bar run writes the catalog. USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1336-1338: "The cheap bar regenerates the catalog in memory and fails on any byte of difference". "In memory" rules out a write. :1476-1477 says each mutation case "must fail the check with its own message", and the case list includes "an edited catalog list" (:1483). Code that writes the catalog on every Run and then compares would turn that case green, so it fails the preregistered suite. The claimed scenario, "a check that always passes against itself" with a .cs rewrite mid-bar, is therefore excluded by the block's own text. "It writes the catalog" (:1145, :1328 "written by UsVetoDrawCheck") names who generates the file: the instrument, not a Tools/ script. That contrasts with Generated/PolishVetoRates.cs, whose line 1 reads "GENERATED by Tools/veto_b1_backtest.pl". It does not say the cheap-bar Run writes. (2) Document. The block does state a byte-level reproduction. :1477 says "the control reproduces the document and the catalog byte for byte". The document never enters the decision. THE DECISION reads only the exact LOO figures (:1219-1220). The ruling record passes only "while the generated outcome and the decisive digest equal the registered ones" (:1459-1460), and the decisive digest excludes every printed-only figure (:1448-1449). So write-or-compare for the .md cannot change an outcome or digest. (3) The plan's own convention fixes the remaining mechanics for any honest implementer: - :136 says "a committed Tools/ script writes them and a check re-reads them". - :289 (US-10) says "a check fails the cheap bar when it goes stale". - Every sibling check uses compare-in-Run with a separate writer refused while any check fails: UsHouseCountCheck.cs ~720-723 / 731-751, UsSenateRaceCheck.cs:924/952, UsStateSwingCheck.cs:69/100, UsNationalVoteCheck.cs:121/150, PlaySheetCheck.cs:31, D18Inventory.Emit. - The preregistered R-US18 and R-US19 blocks (:1516-1728, :1756-1993) also leave the write entry point unstated, and were implemented in that form without ambiguity. Naming a Write method would be a clarity tweak at most (low), not a medium ambiguity that changes what the rule decides. Aside, not this claim: the parenthetical "(Generated.PolishVetoRates' pattern)" at :1338 does not match its reference. PresidentialVetoDiagnostic.cs:233-249 recomputes the table's rows and compares the stored digests; it does not regenerate the file in memory byte for byte.

### game-F7 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** 8. A projection reads exact probabilities and never draws. Only the gate draws.

**Defect.** The block does not say where in play the draw happens, and the code base's only gate cannot hold it. Item 6 has the drawn crossers 'enter a chamber's recorded division only where they change its outcome', so the draw must be made before ParliamentSystem.RecordDivision and can turn a failed chamber vote into a passed one. In the code, the vote sites compute 'passed' with WouldBillPass, which is the projection and must not draw, then call RecordDivision. PresidentialVetoGate runs only afterwards and returns at once on '!passed'. 'Only the gate draws' read as a veto gate (the block leaves 'the gate's name' to US-21) makes item 6 impossible: such a gate never sees a failed passage, and the division is already written.

**Evidence.** SimulationManager.cs:1307-1310: 'bool passed = ParliamentSystem.WouldBillPass(country, concern); ... DivisionRecord vote = ParliamentSystem.RecordDivision(country, title, concern, passed, CurrentDate); passed = PresidentialVetoGate(country, vote, passed);'. Line 1454: 'if (!passed ...) { return passed; }'. US-18: 'ParliamentSystem.WouldBillPass and ChamberVerdicts read both chambers for the USA through one projection'.

**Fix proposed.** Add: 'THE GATE is a chamber-division gate at each US ordinary-bill vote site, after the bloc projection (WouldBillPass, which never draws) and before RecordDivision, which records the drawn crossers where they change the outcome. It is not PresidentialVetoGate, which runs after passage. The veto (rule (a)) reads the division the gate recorded.'

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The code the reviewer quotes is accurate, but the block already says where the draw happens, so this is not a defect of the rule. 1. **The draw comes before the chamber outcome and the veto.**    - Item 5 (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:873-874): "ONE DRAW per party per chamber per bill serves every vote of that chamber on it (cloture, passage, concurrence)". A draw that serves cloture is made at the chamber's own division, not after passage.    - Item 6 (876-878): the crossers enter "a chamber's recorded division only where they change its outcome".    - WHEN THE DRAW MATTERS gives play's own passage test with the crossers in it: House "Play: passes iff Y > N among the seated members, every one voting his bloc's side or as a crosser" (954-955), and the Senate's play base (965).    - THE PLAY KEY (1112) dates the draw at "THAT CHAMBER'S FIRST DIVISION on the bill".    - THE VETO (1002-1003) is decisive: "The draw decides whether a bill REACHES the president, never whether (a) fires." Line 999 reads the veto's nays as "S_PP,H - Kp" in "the chamber's division", so the veto reads a division the draw has already shaped.    - Item 4 moves the PP's crossers from nay to yea, so the draw can turn a failed vote into a passed one. No post-passage gate could do that.    - Two honest implementers therefore cannot put the draw in a gate that runs after passage. 2. **The gate cannot be PresidentialVetoGate.** POLAND'S B1/F3 (1298) says "PresidentialVetoGate's body is untouched", and 1302 requires this "in the first-run commit and again at US-21's". The US draw cannot live in that method, so the misreading the reviewer fears is already ruled out. 3. **The wiring is left to US-21 on purpose.** Lines 738-740 leave "the gate's name" and ChamberVerdicts' projection to US-21's text. The vote sites cited (SimulationManager.cs:1307-1310 and 1454) are today's single-chamber code. US-18 is not yet built, and it rebuilds those sites for the USA: "Each chamber records its own division" (plan:421). Today's call order is not the order US-21 will wire into. The proposed fix would freeze a call site, before RecordDivision, into a rule that by design does not decide call sites.

### game-F8 (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** It is read from the index blob's raw bytes and required equal to the working copy's.

**Defect.** On this repository the requirement either fails on every run or is implemented two ways. core.autocrlf is true and the plan is checked out 'i/lf w/crlf', so the working copy's raw bytes always differ from the index blob's. One implementer compares raw bytes and the check is permanently red. Another normalises CRLF to LF, as UsHouseCountCheck does (it reads only the working copy, '.Replace("\r\n", "\n")'). No existing ruled-block check reads the index at all, so there is no precedent to settle it.

**Evidence.** git config core.autocrlf = true; 'git ls-files --eol docs/specs/USA_STAGE_PLAN.md' gives 'i/lf w/crlf'. UsHouseCountCheck.cs:577 reads the working copy with File.ReadAllText(...).Replace("\r\n", "\n") and never reads a git blob. Only ReviewLedgerCheck and UpstreamCheck invoke git.

**Fix proposed.** Write: 'It is read from the index blob (git cat-file over the staged path) and from the working copy with every CRLF replaced by LF; the two block texts must be byte-equal, and the digest is taken over the LF form.' Name which failure message comes first, so the mutation case 'the block edited without a listed digest' fails with its own message rather than 'index differs from working copy'.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quote is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1420-1421), and the line-ending facts are right: core.autocrlf=true, and `git ls-files --eol` reports `i/lf w/crlf` with no .gitattributes entry for docs/. The defect still does not hold, for four reasons. (1) The block already defines the normalised form. The sentence just before the quote (1418-1420) defines the digest over "the lines strictly between the marker lines, fence lines included, each ending LF with no CR". "It ... required equal to the working copy's" compares the working copy's block or digest under that same definition. The block never says to compare the working copy's raw bytes. "Raw bytes" qualifies only the index blob, which is LF anyway. The CRLF-to-LF form the reviewer proposes ("the digest is taken over the LF form") is already in the text. The words "with no CR" only make sense as that normalisation, because the index side never holds a CR. (2) The claim that there is no precedent is false on the side that matters. The R-US18 and R-US19 block checks both read the working copy as `File.ReadAllText(...).Replace("\r\n", "\n")` before digesting: Assets/Editor/UsHouseCountCheck.cs:577 and Assets/Editor/UsSenateRaceCheck.cs:471. That is exactly the "working copy's" reading the rule needs. The index side needs no normalisation, since its blob is LF. (3) A raw-bytes implementation cannot land, so it cannot mis-decide. The first-run commit requires the cheap bar green with UsVetoDrawCheck added (1492-1494). A check that is red on every run is caught at once and fixed by the definition in (1). Any route that turns green gives the same digest, so honest implementers converge on one result rather than two. (4) The second part of the proposed fix (which failure message comes first) is not needed. Checks collect every failure without stopping (UsHouseCountCheck.cs:714 `void Check(bool ok, string what) { if (!ok) { failures++; } ... }`). The mutation suites mark a case CAUGHT when the expected message appears anywhere in the output, via `grep -qF` (Tools/us_house_mutations.sh:40-41, Tools/us_veto_mutations.sh:40). So an extra index-versus-working-copy message never hides a case's own message. At most, adding "in its LF form" after "the working copy's" would be a low-severity clarity edit. It is not a medium defect.

### inference-BOOT-scoring-set (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.**     - TOTALS: taken over the replicate's picks with multiplicity, using those per-measure
      figures. The replicate's V_COUNT is its picked counted vetoes with multiplicity.

**Defect.** For the count figures (E[vetoable sent], E_XXII, E[named], E[hits]) and the P(NONE) products, the default record bootstrap resamples the scoring set as well as the fit. Each replicate holds a random number of V and V_XXII measures, roughly Poisson around |V| and |V_XXII|, and a measure picked twice enters P(NONE) as (1-p)^2. The interval on E_XXII is then mostly about how many Rule XXII bills the replicate happens to contain, not fit uncertainty. Yet it is printed as FITTED beside the fixed record counts "|V_XXII| - all sent on the record", and the verdict line places it next to "(all <|V_XXII|> were sent on the record)". H2's interval would overstate and mislabel the uncertainty of exactly the S2 figure.

**Evidence.** Lines 1066-1073 define the record bootstrap. Lines 1177-1179 print E sums beside fixed |V| and |V_XXII|. The fixed-scoring-set alternative exists only as R17 (lines 1388-1390, "It changes intervals only").

**Fix proposed.** Use R17's cell bootstrap (scoring set fixed to the real V, VETO_j and V_XXII; owners of observations resampled) as the default BOOT for every figure that sums or multiplies over V or V_XXII, and for per-measure figures. Keep record resampling for the ratios (precision, hit rate) or print it as the reading. State in THE PRINT which method each figure uses.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: This is a preference about method, and the block already handles it. It is not a defect. (1) The rule is not ambiguous. USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1064-1078 says exactly how BOOT works: record resampling, M keyed picks, and totals "taken over the replicate's picks with multiplicity". Two honest implementers would compute the same interval. (2) No interval can mis-decide. "The outcome reads only the default's exact LOO figures under (B)" (1219-1220). Every interval is "PRINTED ONLY, never part of the outcome" (1240-1241). The decisive digest excludes intervals too: "Nothing else enters: no interval, reading..." (1448-1449). So S2 is decided on exact E_XXII and P(NONE_XXII) (1229-1231), never on BOOT. Whether the replicate's count of V_XXII varies cannot change a verdict. (3) Record resampling is faithful to H2. H2 gives the thinness as "13 vetoable measures, 25 vetoes" (plan:727). Those are sizes of the scoring set, not of the cells. An interval that carries how few vetoable measures and vetoes the record holds therefore answers H2's own reason. Under the cell bootstrap that the fix proposes, that source of uncertainty would vanish from the default. (4) Nothing is mislabelled under the block's own definitions: - FITTED means "any number computed from the cells' observations" (1045). The label names the method, BOOT, and carries m=|V_XXII| (1057-1058, 1082). That tells the reader the figure is a sum over the scoring set. - "|V_XXII| - all sent on the record" is printed explicitly as "not fitted" (1177-1179). - In the verdict line, "(all <|V_XXII|> were sent on the record)" (1251) is a record count shown beside the value. (5) The alternative is already in the block. R17, the cell bootstrap with "the scoring set fixed (the real V, VETO_j)", is a declared reading that is run and printed (1388-1390). Its key is reserved (1132). At most, the DRAWN form's phrase "not fit uncertainty" (1095-1096) could be read as calling BOOT pure fit uncertainty. That is a low-severity wording point, not the claimed medium defect.

### inference-BOOT-undefined-sort (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** The UNDEFINED values sort first. If k >= 50 the interval prints
      "UNDEFINED IN <k> OF 2000". Otherwise lo = sorted[49] and hi = sorted[1950]

**Defect.** Sorting UNDEFINED replicates first treats them as minus infinity. For 1 <= k < 50 both bounds then shift down by k ranks among the defined values: lo becomes the (49-k)th defined value and hi the (1950-k)th. The result is neither the percentile interval of the defined replicates nor a conservative one. For precision, a replicate is UNDEFINED when its cells lack the high-share observations, so P(sent) = 0 everywhere (E[named] = 0). Those are low-sending worlds, and treating them as the lowest precision is an arbitrary, outcome-correlated choice. Two honest implementers could also differ on whether "sorted" means the 2000 values or the defined ones.

**Evidence.** Lines 1075-1078. UNDEFINED is defined only as a "zero denominator". It applies to precision, hit rate and the mean share of an empty replicate cell.

**Fix proposed.** Take percentiles over the 2000-k defined replicates only, at ranks fixed now (for example lo = sorted_def[floor(0.025*(2000-k))], hi = sorted_def[ceil(0.975*(2000-k))-1], 0-based). Keep the threshold k >= 50 -> "UNDEFINED IN <k> OF 2000" and the appended "(<k> of 2000 replicates undefined)".

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The claim does not hold up. Its ambiguity half is wrong, and its statistical half is a preference between two defensible conventions, not a defect. (1) The text is not ambiguous. USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1076 says "The UNDEFINED values sort first". That only makes sense if the UNDEFINED values are inside the sorted array, so "sorted" can only mean all 2000 replicate values. The indices 49 and 1950 at line 1077 are 2000-based ranks, which confirms it. Two honest implementers would build the same thing. (2) The convention is well defined and standard. A replicate can be UNDEFINED only for a ratio with a zero denominator: - precision = E[hits]/E[named] and hit rate = E[hits]/V_COUNT (line 1160); - the mean share (line 938); - P(override | named) (line 1035). All of these are bounded below by 0. When k < 50, sorted[49] and sorted[1950] are always defined values (49 >= k). So the printed interval is exactly what you get by assigning UNDEFINED the lowest value of the range, 0. That is the common zero-division=0 convention, not an arbitrary minus infinity. (3) It matches the block's own model. Lines 868-870 say an empty list in a bootstrap replicate draws as the single index "K = 0", which is share 0. So scoring a replicate with an empty cell or nothing named at the bottom is consistent with how the model treats that world. (4) The bias argument cuts both ways. The proposed fix drops the k replicates, and those are the low-sending worlds, so the fix biases the interval upward. Neither choice is unbiased. The block's choice leans toward the stop: it lowers both bounds, which makes R13's "S1 on the BOOT upper bound (strict) ... lower bound (lax)" (line 1382) more likely to fire. That is the protective direction for "(a) only if its precision ... is at least B1's". (5) Nothing can be mis-decided. Line 1241 makes every interval "PRINTED ONLY, never part of the outcome". Line 1449 keeps intervals out of the decisive digest ("no interval"). Line 1078 prints k beside every interval it touches, and k >= 50 prints "UNDEFINED IN <k> OF 2000". The quoted text is accurate, but the rule is neither ambiguous nor wrong here. This is a convention choice with no effect on the decision, so it does not meet the bar for a medium defect.

### inference-amend-ruling-ambiguity (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.**   - The block must equal the list's last entry. A change without a ruling fails.

**Defect.** Two passages disagree on who may change the rule after a result is seen. Line 1148-1149 says such a change "takes a new digest and is reported to Elias (G1)", which reads as change first, then report. Lines 1424-1427 require every new entry to be "AMENDED (Hn, sNNN)" naming Elias's ruling, and say a change without a ruling fails. One implementer would amend after seeing figures and report afterwards; another would wait for a ruling. That is the steering door the preregistration exists to close.

**Evidence.** Lines 1148-1149 against lines 1424-1427.

**Fix proposed.** Rewrite line 1148-1149 as: "After the first run, the block changes only by an amendment naming Elias's ruling, made before the instrument runs on it; the outcome under the preregistered block stays registered beside it (G1)."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The two passages fit together; they do not conflict. Lines 1148-1149 ("A choice changed after a result is in sight takes a new digest and is reported to Elias (G1)") nearly copy Elias's ruling G1 word for word. The plan records G1 at line 1743 and again at line 2009: "a result never changes a choice - a choice changed after a result is in sight needs a new digest and a report to Elias." So the sentence is faithful to the ruling. The sentence does not say code may make the change, and it does not say the change can happen without a ruling. It only says such a change "takes a new digest." The block then states, in one place, how a new digest can come about. Lines 1424-1427 say every entry after the first in RuleDigests is "AMENDED (Hn, sNNN): old -> new", naming Elias's ruling. They add: "The block must equal the list's last entry. A change without a ruling fails." Suppose an implementer reads 1148 as "change first, report later." Their edited block would not match the last listed digest, so the instrument fails. It keeps failing until they add a ruling-named entry. The mutation case "the block edited without a listed digest" (line 1484) tests exactly this. One reading is therefore enforced, not two. The rest of the block says the same thing every time: - Line 754-755: a pin changes only by "an amendment naming Elias's ruling". - Line 765: "until the block is amended by a ruling". - Line 1264: each stop's options carry "amend the rule as ruled". - Lines 1468-1469: a ruling must name "(c) amended by name". The "report to Elias" in G1 sits alongside the ruling and does not replace it. Lines 1464-1467 also carry it: a moved digest names its cause and the per-measure P(sent) differences go to Elias. R-US18 and R-US19 used the same G1 wording and recorded their post-ask changes as ruled amendments, "AMENDED (G1, §797)" (lines 1732, 1997). The proposed fix would also add a new condition that G1 never set: "made before the instrument runs on it." That is not needed to remove an ambiguity that does not exist. At most, line 1148 could add a cross-reference to the digest list, which is a low-severity clarity point, not a medium defect.

### inference-first-run-unguarded (medium; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** - The first run is on the block as preregistered (H1, H3). A choice changed after a result
  is in sight takes a new digest and is reported to Elias (G1).

**Defect.** The block freezes its text, not the code that implements it, and nothing stops the instrument from being run on the real CSV many times during development. The "first run" registered at the first-run commit can follow unregistered runs. Every point two implementers could resolve differently, and every code path the block leaves open, can then be settled with the figures in sight, and no record shows it. The only proof required is ordering: "that commit preceding the one that adds UsVetoDrawCheck.cs" (lines 1428-1429).

**Evidence.** The ruling record starts at NONE and "the first-run commit registers the outcome" (lines 1451-1452). No clause requires the code to be frozen before its first execution on the pinned source.

**Fix proposed.** Add: UsVeto.cs and UsVetoDrawCheck.cs are developed and verified only against planted CSVs (the mutation suite's fixtures, whose SourceDigest differs from the SOURCE PIN). The instrument refuses to evaluate the pinned source until a commit records the code blobs. The first execution on the pinned source is the registered first run, and its review row shows those blobs unchanged. Any later code change on the pinned source follows the MOVED DIGEST procedure even when the digest does not move.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quote is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1148-1149), and so are the cited lines 1428-1429 and 1451-1452. It is still not a defect of the rule. (1) The block already says what happens when something is decided with figures in sight. Line 1149 reads "A choice changed after a result is in sight takes a new digest and is reported to Elias (G1)". A result is in sight at any execution on the CSV, development runs included. So if an implementer settles an open point after such a run, the block requires an amendment and a report. Two honest implementers would apply this the same way. The claim does not show an ambiguity: it worries about an unrecorded dishonest run. The proposed fix cannot stop that either, because the refusal it adds lives in code that the same implementer writes. (2) The block is faithful to the rulings and to precedent. H3 (plan:728) asks only that "the new rule is preregistered and its digest taken before its first run". It says nothing about freezing code. The practice G1 set (COMPLETED.md:37802, s800) is that named development runs on the real data come before the registered run ("house800a ... Restructured before anything was registered (house800b)"), and departures are declared. s802 does the same (COMPLETED.md ~37950, "Departures, declared: The verdict changed after a result was seen ... Elias is told"). Once the outcome is registered, the block also tracks code changes: a moved digest must be re-pinned with "its cause (source, code or amendment)" (plan:1464-1467). (3) The fix does not work as stated: - Many of the block's own checks can only be confirmed on the pinned record: SEATS equal US_VETO_BACKTEST.md's table, the US-10 identities, the UNPREDICTABLE set, B1 = 45 of 166, every share <= 1/2, and every roll call dated 2017 or later (plan:807, 1192-1207, 764, 912, 777). The mutation suite's control must also reproduce the document byte for byte (plan:1476-1477). - The suite's "planted CSVs" are mutated copies of the real record, as in Tools/us_veto_mutations.sh's fresh()/case_ pattern. Running on them shows essentially the same figures, so they do not keep anyone blind. - A run on a non-pinned source is a DEFECT anyway (plan:754-755). - A first execution on the pinned source that hits a DEFECT has no outcome to register (plan:736-737). Under the fix, every bug fix after it would become a "later code change" handled by amendment. That clashes with the block's DEFECT and ruling-record machinery. This is a suggestion for tighter process, not a wrong, uncomputable, unfaithful or ambiguous rule.

### computability-C10 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** DRAWN 1000 worlds [<26th>, <975th>]

**Defect.** The ordinal base is not stated, while BOOT states its bounds 0-based ("sorted[49] ... sorted[1950] (0-based)"). "26th" read 1-based is sorted[25], and read as an index it is sorted[26]. The printed spreads differ between the two readings.

**Evidence.** THE PRINT, INTERVALS gives BOOT as explicit 0-based indices. DRAWN FORM uses ordinals.

**Fix proposed.** "[sorted[25], sorted[974]] (0-based, ascending, over the 1000 seeds)".

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quote is accurate: USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1095 reads "DRAWN FORM: \"<mean> DRAWN 1000 worlds [<26th>, <975th>] - seed noise of a single game,". The ambiguity it alleges is not real, for three reasons. 1. An ordinal has no base to state. "26th" means the 26th value in sorted order, which is sorted[25]. Getting sorted[26] out of it means treating an ordinal as an index, and the block never mixes the two. Wherever it means an index it writes index notation and labels it: BOOT at :1077-1078 ("lo = sorted[49] and hi = sorted[1950] (0-based)"), :1067 ("Pick t (0-based)") and :1436 ("i 0-based"). So the BOOT contrast the reviewer cites actually supports the 1-based reading: the block writes sorted[...] when it means an index and writes ordinals otherwise. 2. The numbers settle the reading. Read 1-based, the 26th through 975th of 1000 drop 25 values at each end, which is exactly the central 95% (2.5% per tail). The index reading, sorted[26] and sorted[975], drops 26 below and 24 above, an asymmetric interval that nobody would pick for a 95% spread. The reviewer's own fix, "sorted[25], sorted[974]", is the 1-based reading, so the reviewer resolves the text the only sensible way too. 3. Nothing depends on the spread. DRAWN figures are "printed only" (:1124) and "no seed decides anything" (:1118). The one assertion on the seeded realizations (:1129-1131) uses the mean and the sample sd, not the 26th or 975th value. Even a misreading could not mis-decide anything, and no other spot in the block (:1053-1054, :1163, :1184) gives these positions a second meaning. I searched the repo for a conflicting precedent (the plan, COMPLETED.md, docs, and Assets/Editor C# for "26th", "975th", "worlds [" and "DRAWN") and found none. So this is not a real defect: two honest implementers would land on the same values.

### computability-C13 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** R12: the veto drawn at (a)'s FITTED precision (F3's form): expected vetoes = E[named] * precision.

**Defect.** "(a)'s FITTED precision" is ambiguous. It could be this rule's PRECISION = E[hits]/E[named], or (a)'s precision on the recorded House votes (the companion, which is not fitted). Under the first reading R12's per-measure veto probability P(named_j)*precision leaves the precision unchanged, so S1 cannot change by construction, yet R12 is listed as a reading whose outcome may differ. The per-measure form is also not given.

**Evidence.** PRECISION is defined in TOTALS. The companions are "not fitted" (item 8). THE STOPS' OPTIONS offer "(c) with R12's drawn veto".

**Fix proposed.** "R12: P(veto_j) = P(named_j) * PRECISION (this rule's, LOO, exact). Its precision equals the default's, so its S1 is identical, stated. It changes E[hits] and the hit rate."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quoted text is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1379-1380), but the claimed defect does not hold up. (1) The block's own vocabulary already settles which precision is meant. THE PRINT (lines 1045-1047) defines a FITTED FIGURE as "any number computed from the cells' observations, under the default or any reading", and lists "precision" among them. Lines 1050-1052 list "the companions" as NOT FITTED, "printed plainly as 'x of y'". So "(a)'s FITTED precision" can only mean the rule's PRECISION = E[hits] / E[named] (line 1160), the precision of (a) as it decides under the draw (line 995). It cannot mean the companion's (a) figure on the recorded House votes, because item 8 (lines 1185-1187) says that figure is not fitted. The reviewer's own evidence ("companions are not fitted") is what rules out the second reading. R12 runs "the whole rule with one change" (line 1355), so it inherits the default's exact LOO computation; nothing is left open. (2) "R12 is listed as a reading whose outcome may differ" misquotes the block. Lines 1245-1246 are a generic clause for all readings: "A reading whose outcome differs is named in the verdict line". Line 1258 has a branch for when the outcome is the same under every reading. No line claims that R12 in particular can change the outcome, so a reading that leaves S1 unchanged contradicts nothing. Its headline figures (E[hits], hit rate; line 1213) still change, and that is why it is printed. It is also why the S1 option "(c) with R12's drawn veto" (line 1271) makes sense: it answers S1's "names far more bills than the record vetoed" by scaling the expected vetoes, not by changing the precision. (3) The per-measure form follows from the text. "The veto drawn at ... precision (F3's form)" on a named bill (F3, line 56: "a seeded draw at the president's own rate on the record decides"), together with "expected vetoes = E[named] * precision", allows only P(veto_j) = P(named_j) * precision. (4) The proposed fix would add a false sentence. It says R12's "S1 is identical", but there is an edge case. If PRECISION = 0 exactly with E[named] > 0, the default's S1 fires: 166*0 < 45*E[named] (line 1234). Under R12 the expected vetoes, E[named]*0, are 0, which can make S1 print "not evaluated (denominator 0)" (line 1235). So "identical by construction" is not exactly true. At most this is a clarity preference, not an ambiguity two honest implementers would resolve differently.

### computability-C14 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** S1 on the BOOT upper bound (strict) and on the lower bound (lax).

**Defect.** "Strict" and "lax" do not fix the inequalities, and the comparison operands are not given. It is unclear whether the double bound is compared with 45/166 exactly or with the printed 27.1 %, and whether the test is < or <=. The verdict names readings whose outcome differs, so this matters.

**Evidence.** EXACTNESS covers only THE DECISION's rationals. BOOT bounds are IEEE doubles.

**Fix proposed.** "R13 S1-upper fires iff 166 * hi < 45 (hi as a double); R13 S1-lower fires iff 166 * lo < 45." Then drop the strict/lax labels or define them.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The reading is defined by pointing to S1, and S1 already fixes the operands and the inequality. The block at USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1232-1235 defines S1 as "it fires iff 166 * E[hits] < 45 * E[named]", meaning the precision is strictly below 45/166. R13 at :1381-1382 says "S1 on the BOOT upper bound ... and on the lower bound". Read plainly, that is S1's own test with the bound in place of the point precision: fires iff hi < 45/166, or iff lo < 45/166. The operands are 45 and 166, which is also how B1'S YARDSTICK is defined at :756 ("45 named of 166"). The block "below" convention at :844 ("Equal is never 'below'") reads the same way. The printed 27.1 % is never used to decide anything. It appears only in printed text: the verdict line and the "where 27.1 % lies against the precision's BOOT interval" print at :1241. So no implementer has a textual reason to compare against 0.271. Whether the test is < or <= cannot change any outcome. BOOT bounds are IEEE doubles (:1074, lo = sorted[49], hi = sorted[1950] at :1077). Every double is a dyadic rational (its denominator is a power of 2). 45/166 = 45/(2*83) is not, so no double can equal it. Under an exact comparison, which fits the block's habit of treating a double as its exact value (:1085, "a double rounds half up on its exact decimal value"), < and <= give the same answer. Whatever the inequality, the reading's outcome and its mention in the verdict line come out the same. "Strict" and "lax" are not inequality symbols. They describe how hard the reading's stop is to trigger: on the upper bound, S1 fires only if even the upper bound is below B1 (strict); on the lower bound, it fires as soon as the lower bound is (lax). This is the standard meaning, and it changes no computation. The proposed fix "166 * hi < 45 (hi as a double)" would actually add rounding from double multiplication that the exact reading avoids. At most this is cosmetic wording, not an ambiguity that could make honest implementers reach different outcomes.

### computability-C15 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** PREP PIN: git blob 358eba1c4a3f1dd0af7e65dc91feaae20ba77558 of Tools/us_veto_prep.pl

**Defect.** How the run computes the prep blob is not stated: the index entry, HEAD's tree, git hash-object of the working file (eol-filtered), or a blob hash over the raw working bytes, which a C# check would naturally compute. The repository has core.autocrlf=true, so a fresh checkout can write CRLF into the working copy. The raw-byte blob and the SourceDigest over the CSV's raw bytes would then differ from the pins and fire a false DEFECT. The block's own digest avoids this by being "read from the index blob's raw bytes".

**Evidence.** git config core.autocrlf = true. .gitattributes covers neither Tools/*.pl nor ElectionsData/usa/*.csv. THE RULING RECORD: the block digest is read from the index blob.

**Fix proposed.** "The prep blob is the index entry's object id for Tools/us_veto_prep.pl, and the working copy must hash to it under git's eol normalization. SourceDigest is taken over the index blob's raw bytes of the CSV and required equal to the working copy's bytes, as the block digest is."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The claim's main failure case, a false DEFECT from SourceDigest after a CRLF checkout, cannot happen, and its evidence on this point is wrong. 1. **The CSV is excluded from eol conversion.** The evidence says ".gitattributes covers neither ... ElectionsData/usa/*.csv". That is false. The repository has a second attributes file, ElectionsData/.gitattributes, whose last line is "usa/us_veto_measures.csv -text". Its comment gives the reason: "core.autocrlf would hand a checkout CRLF where the digest was taken over LF" (§786). `git check-attr` reports "ElectionsData/usa/us_veto_measures.csv: text: unset". `git ls-files --eol` shows "i/lf w/lf attr/-text". A checkout therefore writes the stored bytes unchanged, and the block's "SourceDigest = SHA-256 over its raw bytes" (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:748) always equals the SOURCE PIN (line 752). 2. **Raw-byte digests of this CSV already work.** UsVetoBacktestCheck.cs:41-42 takes `File.ReadAllBytes` of this CSV and runs Sha256Of on the bytes. The proposed "index blob raw bytes, required equal to the working copy" clause for SourceDigest fixes nothing. 3. **The prep pin is not ambiguous.** Line 753 says "PREP PIN: git blob 358eba1c... of Tools/us_veto_prep.pl". A git blob id is defined by git. The three readings the claim lists (the index entry, HEAD's tree, `git hash-object` on the working file) all give the same id for an unmodified file. That holds because `git hash-object` applies the path's eol conversion, so under core.autocrlf=true a CRLF working copy hashes to the same LF blob. A SHA-1 taken over raw CRLF bytes is not "the git blob of" that path, so code that computed it would be an implementation bug, not a gap in the rule. Editor checks can already call git (UpstreamCheck.cs:39 uses TryGit). 4. **Uncommitted edits are already covered.** The only real difference between readings, an edited but uncommitted prep file, is handled elsewhere in the block. Line 1277-1280 says "Tools/us_veto_prep.pl ... byte-identical" at the commit bars, and "re-run whenever the prep tool changes, followed by this instrument". Residue: at most a cosmetic note. Line 753 could add "(git's object id, as `git hash-object` gives it)". That is not a defect that would mis-decide or leave the rule uncomputable.

### computability-C8 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** failed at K = 0

**Defect.** The selection count ("how many PP-NAY roll calls failed at K = 0 on their recorded bloc result") and R6 ("recorded bloc result at K = 0 already passed") do not say which procedure judges a Senate roll call. It could be this block's (A), with the reconciliation MAJORITY path and the Vice President, or US-10 MODE, with neither. For a shared roll call the text also does not say whose kind applies: the owner's, or the measure's. R6's outcome is named in the verdict line.

**Evidence.** WHEN THE DRAW MATTERS defines (A) with the Vice President and SENATE PROCEDURE with a reconciliation path, while US-10 MODE has neither. THE FIT and R6 name neither.

**Fix proposed.** "Judged by (A) at K = 0 under the OWNER's SENATE PROCEDURE, with the Vice President as in WHEN THE DRAW MATTERS."

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quote is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:902-903; R6 at :1368-1369), but the block already settles each point the claim calls open. (1) US-10 MODE is not a possible reading. Line 987 limits it outright: "US-10 MODE (used only by THE US-10 IDENTITIES)". The selection count (:900-904) and R6 (:1368) are not those identities. The only other judging procedure the block defines is (A) per chamber, and WHEN THE DRAW MATTERS is headed "the margin test per chamber and procedure" (:942). It takes the procedure from SENATE PROCEDURE (:822-824), which THE TERMS define for every counted measure, reconciliation MAJORITY path included. "Recorded bloc result" is (A)'s own test: "over the R and D blocs as the roll call lists them" (:952, :958, :965). (2) The Vice President cannot change either verdict. Both the selection count and R6's PP cells read only PP-NAY roll calls. The Vice President "breaks a tie with the PP's stance in that chamber" (:959), which on those roll calls is NAY, so a tie fails with him or without him (Y > N is strict). Under Rule XXII "the Vice President has no cloture vote" (:967-968). So two implementers who differ on whether he sits in (A) still get identical counts. (3) A shared roll call is covered. Lines 775-776 say "A roll call final for several counted measures is ONE roll call everywhere. Its OWNER is the first of them in CSV row order". Line 905 takes the fitted (PP-NAY) roll calls by "the PP of its OWNER". The selection count counts those same distinct roll calls, so the owner's measure, and with it its SENATE PROCEDURE, governs. A per-measure reading would give one roll call two verdicts, which "ONE roll call everywhere" rules out. So the proposed fix only restates what lines 775-776, 822-824, 905, 959-968 and 987 already fix. It is not a defect of the rule.

### consistency-IC-11 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** **Ruling record:** ASKED (sNNN): S1 + S2.

**Defect.** The mirror forms do not cover every state of the ruling record. (1) An ASKED registered for a changed outcome carries 'the old and new outcomes', but the form shows one outcome, and an ASKED whose new outcome is INSTALL or S3 has no form. (2) The record says the mirror holds 'the state, section and ruling', yet the RULED form carries only '<option>', not Elias's ruling. (3) The outcome is written 'S1 + S2' in THE OUTCOME and the mirror but 'S1+S2' in the digest. (4) 'sNNN' versus the plan's '§NNN': the line after the block already reads 'NONE (§832)', and the R-US18/R-US19 mirrors use '§'.

**Evidence.** THE RULING RECORD 'A CHANGED OUTCOME ... registered ASKED with the old and new outcomes'; 'RULED (Elias's ruling, sNNN), with its option'; digest 'outcome=INSTALL|S3|S1|S2|S1+S2'; plan line 1503 '**Ruling record:** NONE (§832)'; UsHouseCountCheck.cs line 690 mirror format.

**Fix proposed.** Give the full grammar: '**Ruling record:** ASKED (§NNN): <outcome>[, was <old outcome>].' and '**Ruling record:** RULED (§NNN; <ruling>): <option>.', and state that 'sNNN' renders as '§NNN' in the mirror. Use one spelling of the combined outcome ('S1 + S2') in the digest too, or say that the digest writes it without spaces.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: I checked each of the four parts against the block and against the R-US18/R-US19 precedent it copies. None of them leads to a different mirror or a different decision. (1) The quote is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1473). The mirror is defined as holding "the state, section and ruling, never a digest" (1470). It never promises to carry the old outcome. The block's state list says ASKED is registered "with the outcome and the digest" (1456). Old and new outcomes live in the record (1461-1462), and the bars compare against the current one (1459-1460). - "S1 + S2" reads as one instance of the outcome slot. The precedent code builds that slot from the registered outcome for any outcome: UsHouseCountCheck.cs:690 and UsSenateRaceCheck.cs:670 use `F("**Ruling record:** {0} ({1}): {2}.", RulingState, RulingSection, RulingOutcomeOverD)`. That gives plan lines 1730 and 1995. - An ASKED for INSTALL, S3, S1 or S2 therefore mirrors the same way. Nobody would hard-code "S1 + S2". (2) "Ruling" in the mirror means the ruled option. The same phrase is in R-US18 (1691) and R-US19 (1954-1955), and their code mirrors RULED as `RULED ({0}): {1}` with RulingRuled. That matches the block's "<option>" (1474). "RULED (Elias's ruling, sNNN)" (1457) names the state, not mirror text. (3) The digest template spells the line out exactly: "outcome=INSTALL|S3|S1|S2|S1+S2" (1447). The decisive digest is not preregistered. The first-run commit registers whatever the instrument computes (1451-1453, 1459-1460), so the spelling is self-consistent and cannot mis-decide. (4) The block has to be "plain ASCII" (1418), so it cannot write "§". It defines "sNNN" as a stand-in for "the COMPLETED.md section a later commit takes" (737-738) and writes "section 832" / "s832" throughout. The precedent instruments hold the section as "§800" / "§801" (UsHouseCountCheck.cs:40, UsSenateRaceCheck.cs:39), and the plan line after the block already reads "§832" (1503). The instrument also fails whenever the mirror and the record differ (1475), and the mutation case "the mirror line changed" (1485) holds either way. No mis-decision is possible. What remains is a wording preference, not a defect two honest implementers would resolve differently.

### consistency-IC-12 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** R12: the veto drawn at (a)'s FITTED precision (F3's form): expected vetoes = E[named] *

**Defect.** R12's headline formula is an identity of the default. Since PRECISION = E[hits] / E[named], E[named] * precision = E[hits]. Under a drawn veto at rate p, expected hits become E[hits]*p and named-vetoed E[named]*p, so precision is unchanged and R12's outcome can never differ from the default. Item 12's headline figures (E[hits], precision, hit rate) are also undefined under R12, since 'expected vetoes' is a new quantity. S1's option '(c) with R12's drawn veto' is therefore printed as a remedy that leaves S1's own test unchanged.

**Evidence.** THE FIRST RUN 'PRECISION = E[hits] / E[named]'; R12; THE STOPS' OPTIONS 'S1: install (c) as measured; (c) with R12's drawn veto'.

**Fix proposed.** Define R12's figures explicitly (expected vetoes = E[named]*p, expected hits = E[hits]*p, precision unchanged, hit rate = E[hits]*p / V_COUNT). State that R12 cannot move S1 and is offered as a play behaviour, not a cure for the statistic.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The arithmetic is right but it is not a defect. (1) The identity is what F3's form is for. Poland's F3 draws the veto at the record's rate of vetoes among statutes at risk (PresidentialVeto.cs:22-25, 57-59), so that expected vetoes in play match the record's hits instead of every bill named. R12 at USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1379-1380 ("the veto drawn at (a)'s FITTED precision (F3's form): expected vetoes = E[named] * precision") prints exactly that play-side figure: E[named]*precision = E[hits], not E[named]. (2) A reading whose outcome cannot differ is allowed. Readings are "never a stop" (1355-1356, 1245-1246). Other readings also cannot move the outcome by design: R15 changes only override prints, which are "PRINTED ONLY, never deciding" (1032, 1384-1385), and R17 "changes intervals only" (1390). (3) Item 12's headline figures are defined under R12. E[hits], precision and hit rate are given by formulas over P(named_j) (1156-1160). NAMED is "sent, and rule (a) fires" (836), and R12 leaves that unchanged because it draws the veto only on a bill already named. So the default figures carry over literally, and R12 adds one figure that it defines explicitly. (4) S1's options (1271) are rulings for Elias, not re-tests that must make S1 pass. S2's option "install (c) as measured" (1267) leaves its test unchanged as well. RULED (Elias's ruling, sNNN), "with its option, the outcome it was made on and the digest" (1457-1460), keeps the stopped outcome on record. The drawn veto answers the substance of the STOP, "names far more bills than the record vetoed" (721), in play, because expected vetoes fall to E[hits] <= V_COUNT. A sentence saying "R12 changes no outcome", like R17's, would be a clarity nicety only.

### consistency-IC-13 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** (iii) (a) on the recorded House votes reproduces the document's (a) pooled row;

**Defect.** THE VETO defines (a) on the bloc division and asserts 'in every run, reading and replicate' that (a) fires exactly where the PP's House stance is NAY. Identity (iii) computes (a) on recorded nays against members (US-10's 2*nay > members), where that identity does not hold. An implementer who calls UsVeto.NamedByA with the bloc division for (iii) will not reproduce US-10's row.

**Evidence.** THE VETO 'IDENTITY, asserted in every run, reading and replicate'; us_veto_backtest.pl line 95 ('2 * $s->[1] > $mem', recorded nays over all four columns).

**Fix proposed.** Write (iii) as: '(a) as US-10 reads it, 2 * recorded PP House nay > PP House members (all four columns), not the bloc division; the IDENTITY of THE VETO does not apply to it'.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The block already covers this. The words of (iii), USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1200 "(a) on the recorded House votes reproduces the document's (a) pooled row", tell the implementer to read the record's House votes, not the model's bloc division. Everywhere else in the backtest the block uses "recorded" for the CSV's own figures: "the OP's recorded stance" (l.1201), "recorded bloc result" (l.903), "as recorded on that roll call" (l.913). The row to be matched also fixes the formula. US-10's (a) is us_veto_backtest.pl:95: 2 * recorded PP House nay > members summed over all four columns. THE VETO's base under (A) is already "its House members as the roll call lists them ((A); US-10's member count)" (l.997-998). Putting the recorded nays into l.995's "2 * nays > S_PP,H" gives exactly that formula, and no other reading reproduces the row. So an implementer who used the bloc division would fail the identity's own assertion at once; two honest implementers cannot settle on different computations. There is no clash with THE VETO either. The IDENTITY at l.1001 is scoped "under the cap": it holds because nays = S_PP,H - Kp with Kp <= floor((S-1)/2) (l.864, l.999). That is a statement about drawn divisions, and recorded votes carry no cap. Item 10 also lists that identity as its own bullet (l.1204), apart from the US-10 IDENTITIES. Those are run in US-10 MODE (l.987-989, l.1194) and printed as "US-10's record (s802), not this rule's figure". Item 8 shows the same intent: US-10 comparisons use "us_veto_backtest.pl's own definitions" (l.1185-1188). The reviewer's own summary concedes the point, since it says (iii) "computes (a) on recorded nays against members", which is what the text already says. The proposed rewording would at most add clarity; nothing in the rule needs fixing.

### consistency-IC-14 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** DRAWN FORM: "<mean> DRAWN 1000 worlds [<26th>, <975th>]

**Defect.** The two percentile conventions disagree. BOOT takes sorted[49] and sorted[1950] of 2000 (49 values outside each end). DRAWN takes the 26th and 975th of 1000 (25 outside each end, which matches BOOT's convention only if it were indices 24 and 975). DRAWN also does not say whether the ordinals are 1-based, while BOOT states 0-based.

**Evidence.** THE PRINT BOOT 'lo = sorted[49] and hi = sorted[1950] (0-based)' vs DRAWN FORM '[<26th>, <975th>]'.

**Fix proposed.** State DRAWN as 0-based indices on the same convention as BOOT, e.g. 'lo = sorted[24], hi = sorted[975] (0-based, seed order sorted ascending)', or align BOOT to DRAWN's.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quotes are accurate. USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1095 reads "<mean> DRAWN 1000 worlds [<26th>, <975th>]", and :1077-1078 reads "lo = sorted[49] and hi = sorted[1950] (0-based)". But the claim does not describe a defect in the rule. (1) The DRAWN form is not ambiguous. "26th" and "975th" are English ordinals, which count from 1. There is no 0-based reading of an ordinal word; on that reading the first value would be the "0th". BOOT says "(0-based)" because it uses bracketed array indices, not ordinals. Ascending order is implied by the [lo, hi] bracket: a descending reading would print [high, low]. So "26th" means sorted[25] and "975th" means sorted[974]. That leaves 25 values outside each end, a symmetric interval that is the textbook 95% rank convention. (2) It is true that the two intervals use different conventions: BOOT leaves 49 of 2000 outside each tail (2.45%), DRAWN 25 of 1000 (2.5%). But each is well defined and symmetric, and nothing in the block says they must match. (3) Nothing depends on the DRAWN interval: - :1053 marks DRAWN as "not fitted; a check". - :1124 says "printed only". - :1118 says "THE BACKTEST'S DECISION FIGURES are EXACT, and no seed decides anything". - The only assertion on the realizations uses the mean and sd, not these percentiles: "|mean - exact| <= 4 * sd / sqrt(1000)" (:1129-1131). - :1449 keeps every interval and realization out of the decisive digest: "Nothing else enters: no interval, reading, (A), override, realization...". - The pins (:750-755) are only the source and prep hashes. Two honest implementers would compute the same DRAWN interval, and no outcome, digest or pin can change. A cosmetic difference between two separately well-defined, print-only intervals does not make the rule wrong, uncomputable or ambiguous.

### consistency-IC-15 (low; upheld 0 of 1)

**Quote.** S1 on the BOOT upper bound (strict) and on the lower bound (lax).

**Defect.** 'Strict' and 'lax' can be read two ways: as the evidence bar for stopping, or as the inequality type ('<' versus '<='). Read the first way, the labels also invert the usual sense, since the upper-bound test fires less often than the default.

**Evidence.** R13; THE DECISION 'S1 ... fires iff 166 * E[hits] < 45 * E[named]'.

**Fix proposed.** Write them out: 'S1 fires iff the BOOT hi < 45/166 (harder to stop); S1 fires iff the BOOT lo < 45/166 (easier to stop)'.

**The skeptics:**

- REFUTED: The quote is accurate (USA_STAGE_PLAN.md:1382: "S1 on the BOOT upper bound (strict) and on the lower bound (lax)"), but it is not a real defect. (1) The labels cannot change what gets computed. R13 names which bound replaces the point figure, and S1's own comparison is given once: "fires iff 166 * E[hits] < 45 * E[named]" (line 1234), which is precision < 45/166. Putting the named bound in its place gives hi < 45/166 for the first reading and lo < 45/166 for the second. Those are exactly the inequalities the proposed fix writes out, so the fix adds no information the block lacks. (2) The claimed inversion is false. The subject of the phrase is S1, the stop. Firing only when the whole interval sits below B1 is the strict bar for stopping; firing when any part of it does is the lax one. The reviewer's own fix glosses them the same way ("harder to stop" / "easier to stop"), so the block's labels already match the meaning the reviewer wants. The "usual sense" the reviewer invokes takes the point of view of installing the candidate, which is not the phrase's subject. (3) The inequality-type reading ("<" against "<=") has no practical effect. It would differ only if a bootstrap percentile, a double read at sorted[49] or sorted[1950] (lines 1076-1077), came out exactly equal to 45/166. Even then it could not mis-decide, because declared readings are "printed; never a stop" (lines 1355-1356). Lines 1245-1246 add that "no reading ever stops the rule" and only name a differing reading in the verdict line.
